# Live Armenian → English transcription

Listens to a call, transcribes spoken Armenian, translates it to English and prints each phrase in the
terminal with time and speaker. Your microphone is labelled `Me`; people on the call side are told apart as
`Speaker 1`, `Speaker 2`, … All models run locally.

```
[00:01:12] Speaker 1  (2.1s · wait 0.0 · asr 1.3 · spk 0.1 · mt 0.2)
HY: Բարև, ինչպե՞ս ես։
EN: Hi, how are you?

[00:01:15] Me  (1.8s · wait 0.0 · asr 1.2 · mt 0.2)
HY: Լավ եմ, շնորհակալություն։
EN: I'm fine, thank you.
```

The grey part of each header is the measured latency from the end of the phrase to the printed line,
followed by the time per stage (queue wait, ASR, speaker embedding, translation). It turns red above 5 s.

> **Status:** code and unit tests are complete. Model choice and thresholds are marked *provisional* until
> they have been measured on the target Mac (see [Measuring](#measuring) and `DECISIONS.md`).

---

## 1. Setup (macOS, Apple Silicon)

### 1.1 Python environment
```bash
python3.11 -m venv .venv
source .venv/bin/activate
pip install -r requirements.txt
```
`sounddevice` ships its own PortAudio on macOS; no Homebrew packages are needed for Python.

### 1.2 Download the models (once, needs internet)
```bash
python scripts/download_models.py                 # default ASR + translation + speaker + VAD
python scripts/download_models.py --asr all       # also the other ASR candidates, for benchmarking
```
After this the program runs **offline**: it sets `HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1` at start, so a missing model is an error,
never a silent download. (`--allow-download` turns that off.)

### 1.3 Capture system audio with BlackHole
macOS cannot record its own output, so the call audio is routed through a virtual loopback device.

1. Install BlackHole 2ch: `brew install blackhole-2ch` (or the installer from existential.audio). Reboot if it
   does not appear.
2. Open **Audio MIDI Setup** → `+` (bottom left) → **Create Multi-Output Device**. Tick your headphones/speakers
   **and** `BlackHole 2ch`. Put your headphones first (primary/clock device) and enable **Drift Correction** on
   BlackHole.
3. Select the **Multi-Output Device** as the output: either in **System Settings → Sound → Output**, or only in
   the call app's speaker setting. You still hear the call; BlackHole gets a copy.
   Note: the volume keys don't control a Multi-Output Device. Set the volume on the headphones first.
4. Keep your real microphone as the call app's input.
5. Use **headphones**. Otherwise the call is played out loud, the mic picks it up, and it is transcribed twice
   (echo handling is out of scope).

Check the routing before starting:
```bash
python -m livetr --list-devices          # find the indices / names
python -m livetr --meter                 # talk -> 'mic' bar moves; play a video -> 'system' bar moves
```

## 2. Run
```bash
python -m livetr                                   # default mic + "BlackHole" device
python -m livetr --mic "MacBook" --system 3        # pick devices by name part or index
python -m livetr --asr buzz                        # another ASR model
python -m livetr --file call.wav                   # replay a recording in real time (no devices needed)
python -m livetr --no-mic                          # only the call side
```
Ctrl-C stops and prints session statistics (latency p50/p90/max per source, time per stage, dropped segments).

Useful knobs: `--min-silence-ms` (pause that ends a phrase, default 500), `--max-segment-s` (forced cut,
default 8), `--speaker-threshold` (default 0.45), `--vad-threshold` (default 0.5). Run `--help` for all of them.

## 3. Architecture

```
 mic ──► DeviceSource ─┐                                      ┌─► ASR (Whisper fine-tune, MPS)
                       ├─► Converter ─► Segmenter (Silero VAD) ├─► Speaker: mic → "Me" / system → ECAPA + online clustering
 BlackHole ► DeviceSource┘   48k→16k mono    per source   queue ─┤    (one worker thread)
                                                              └─► Translation (NLLB-600M, CTranslate2 int8, CPU) ─► terminal
```

| Module | Role | In → out |
|---|---|---|
| `livetr/audio/devices.py` | find devices by index or name | name/index → device index (error if 0 or 2+ matches) |
| `livetr/audio/capture.py` | live device or WAV file; down-mix + streaming resample | device → 16 kHz mono float32 blocks |
| `livetr/segmenter.py` | VAD state machine | blocks → `Segment` (audio + end-of-phrase time) |
| `livetr/asr.py` | candidate ASR models behind one interface | audio → Armenian text |
| `livetr/speakers.py` | `Me` / embeddings / online clustering | segment → label |
| `livetr/translate.py` | NLLB via CTranslate2 | Armenian → English |
| `livetr/output.py` | formatting, colours, latency stats | result → terminal |
| `livetr/pipeline.py` | threads and queues | wires everything |

Design points:
- **Utterance-level, not word streaming.** The VAD cuts at a 500 ms pause; each phrase is transcribed whole.
  Whisper is much more accurate on full phrases, and a phrase still fits in 5 s: VAD 0.5 s, then ASR, speaker
  and translation. Phrases longer than 8 s are force-cut so ASR time stays bounded.
- **One worker for both sources.** Two copies of the models do not fit in 8 GB RAM. If both sides talk at the
  same time, one waits; the wait is shown in each line (`wait`).
- **Order inside the worker:** ASR first. If nothing is recognised, nothing is printed and the segment does
  not influence the speaker clusters. Then speaker, then translation.
- **Fail loudly.** A wrong device name, a missing model or an error in any thread stops the program with a clear
  message. No silent fallbacks, retries or timeouts that could hide where a problem is.
- Models are **warmed up** at start so the first real phrase is not slow.

## 4. Models and why

Target machine: MacBook Apple Silicon with **8 GB RAM**. Memory is the main constraint, then the 5 s budget.

### ASR (Armenian speech → text): decision by measurement
Zero-shot Whisper is weak on Armenian, so only Armenian fine-tunes are serious candidates.

| key | model | why consider it | risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| `buzz` | `BuzzASR/armenian` (Whisper large-v3 full fine-tune) | best published numbers among open models (card: FLEURS WER 11.9 %, Common Voice WER 2.4 %) | ~3 GB in fp16 on an 8 GB Mac; large-v3 decoder is slower |
| `turbo-hy` | `Chillarmo/whisper-large-v3-turbo-armenian` | turbo has 4 decoder layers → much faster, ~1.6 GB | no trustworthy published WER, must measure |
| `mms` | `facebook/mms-1b-all` + Armenian adapter | CTC: fast, cannot loop or hallucinate long text | usually less accurate, no punctuation |
/Library/Frameworks/Python.framework/Versions/3.12/bin/python3
python3.11 not found

Python 3.12.4
````

BlackHole is installed and the program finds it as input `[0] BlackHole 2ch`. The **Multi-Output Device doesn't exist yet**: sound still goes straight to MacBook Pro Speakers, so BlackHole gets no audio.

Do the steps you pasted, in **Audio MIDI Setup**:

2. Tick **MacBook Pro Speakers** (or your headphones) and **BlackHole 2ch**.
3. Set the speakers/headphones as **Primary Device** at the top, and tick **Drift Correction** only on BlackHole 2ch.
4. In **System Settings → Sound → Output**, choose **Multi-Output Device**.

Then test it:
```bash
source .venv/bin/activate
python -m livetr --meter
```
Talk, and the `mic` bar should move. Play a YouTube video, and the `system` bar should move.

For real calls, use **headphones**. With the MacBook speakers, your mic hears the other person too, and every line is transcribed twice.

README.md
## 5. Measuring

Model and threshold choices come from numbers, not impressions. All of this runs on the Mac, and audio stays
local (`eval/data/` is git-ignored).

```bash
# 1. record the eval set: 24 sentences from eval/sentences.tsv (+ free speech for speaker tests)
python eval/record.py --speaker anna
python eval/record.py --speaker davit
python eval/record.py --speaker davit --free 6

# 2. ASR: CER/WER, latency and peak memory per model (one process per model)
for m in turbo-hy buzz mms turbo-zeroshot; do python eval/run_asr.py --model $m; done
python eval/run_asr.py --summary

# 3. translation of the reference sentences, and of real ASR output (error propagation)
python eval/run_translate.py
python eval/run_translate.py --from-asr turbo-hy

# 4. speakers: same- vs different-speaker similarity, best threshold, simulated live clustering
python eval/run_speakers.py

# 5. end-to-end latency on a recording, without a call
python -m livetr --file some_call.wav
```

Unit tests (no models or audio devices needed): `pytest`. What is tested and what is checked by hand
is listed in `TESTS.md`.

## 6. Known limitations
- **Accuracy depends on the ASR fine-tune's training data** (read speech: Common Voice, FLEURS). Fast
  conversational speech, dialects, Western Armenian and code-switching with Russian or English will be worse.
- **Call audio is compressed** by the call app (Zoom/Meet codecs, noise suppression). This hurts both ASR and
  speaker embeddings compared with the clean clips in the eval set.
- **Speaker separation is per utterance.** If two people speak in one segment (interruptions, overlap), the
  segment gets one label. Similar voices (same gender, similar age) can be merged into one speaker. Very short
  replies show as `Speaker ?`.
- **One worker.** When both sides talk at the same time, one waits. That wait counts towards the 5 s.
- **Phrase-level output.** Nothing is printed until the speaker pauses (or 8 s pass). Long sentences that are
  force-cut are translated in pieces, so the translation can be worse at the cut.
- **Whisper can still hallucinate** on noise that the VAD lets through (music, typing). There is no text filter,
  on purpose: such errors stay visible.
- **8 GB RAM.** Large models plus a call app and a browser can cause swapping, which shows up as latency spikes.
  Check Activity Monitor → Memory → Swap Used.
- No echo handling: use headphones.

## 7. What I would improve with more time
- Measure and quantise further: int8 Whisper via CTranslate2 (faster-whisper) for the turbo fine-tune, or
  mlx-whisper, to cut ASR latency and memory.
- Fine-tune on conversational Armenian audio recorded through a real call codec.
- Stream partial results (show Armenian text while the person is still speaking, finalise at the pause).
- Process both sources in parallel if memory allows, or prioritise the source that has waited longer.
- Better diarization on the call side: overlap-aware segmentation (pyannote/diart) inside long segments, and
  re-clustering of the first minutes once more data is available.
- Translate with context (previous sentence) for pronouns and cut sentences.
- A small labelled set of real call recordings for regression testing of every change.

## 8. Repository layout
```
livetr/            program (python -m livetr)
eval/              recording + benchmark scripts, sentence list, results (no audio)
scripts/           model download
tests/             pytest unit tests
PLAN.md            plan written before coding
DECISIONS.md       decision log with reasons
TESTS.md           requirement-driven test cases and QA checklist
CLAUDE.md          project rules for AI-assisted work
chats/             exported AI chat transcripts (one .md per session)
```

s01	Բարև, ինչպե՞ս ես։	Hi, how are you?

```
usage: record.py [-h] --speaker SPEAKER [--mic MIC] [--free FREE]

options:
  -h, --help         show this help message and exit
  --speaker SPEAKER  short name of the person speaking, e.g. anna
  --mic MIC          input device index or name (default: system default)
  --free FREE        record N free-speech clips instead of the sentence list
"""Record the evaluation set: one WAV per sentence, 16 kHz mono 16-bit, plus a manifest.

    python eval/record.py --speaker anna                 # reads eval/sentences.tsv
    python eval/record.py --speaker anna --mic 2
    python eval/record.py --speaker davit --free 6       # 6 free-speech clips (no reference) for speaker tests

Output: eval/data/<speaker>/<id>.wav and lines in eval/data/manifest.tsv (path, speaker, reference).
Audio stays local: eval/data/ is in .gitignore.
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import csv
import queue
import sys
import threading
import wave
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
sys.path.insert(0, str(ROOT))

from livetr.audio import devices as dev  # noqa: E402
from livetr.audio.capture import Converter, DeviceSource  # noqa: E402

DATA = ROOT / "eval" / "data"


def record_one(src: DeviceSource) -> np.ndarray:
    conv = Converter(src.sample_rate)
    input("  press Enter to START recording")
    while not src.blocks.empty():
        src.blocks.get()
    chunks: list[np.ndarray] = []
    stop = threading.Event()

    def wait_enter() -> None:
        input("  recording… press Enter to STOP")
        stop.set()

    t = threading.Thread(target=wait_enter, daemon=True)
    t.start()
    while not stop.is_set() or not src.blocks.empty():
        try:
            chunks.append(conv(src.blocks.get(timeout=0.1).audio))
        except queue.Empty:
            continue
    return np.concatenate(chunks) if chunks else np.zeros(0, dtype=np.float32)


def save_wav(path: Path, audio: np.ndarray) -> None:
    path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    pcm = (np.clip(audio, -1, 1) * 32767).astype(np.int16)
    with wave.open(str(path), "wb") as w:
        w.setnchannels(1)
        w.setsampwidth(2)
        w.setframerate(16000)
        w.writeframes(pcm.tobytes())
```

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 15:33:19

Recording comes first. You need at least **2 people** (you plus one friend; 3+ is better for the speaker tests), and each person needs about 15 minutes in a quiet room.

**1. Check the sentences first.** Have a native speaker read the 24 lines in `eval/sentences.tsv` and fix anything that sounds unnatural. If you record first, you'd have to re-record the fixed lines.

**2. Record.** Open Terminal in the project folder and run:
```bash
source .venv/bin/activate
python eval/record.py --speaker me --mic "MacBook"
python eval/record.py --speaker friend1 --mic "MacBook"
python eval/record.py --speaker friend1 --mic "MacBook" --free 6
python eval/record.py --speaker me --mic "MacBook" --free 6
```
- `--speaker` is just a short name, with no spaces.
- `--mic "MacBook"` makes sure it records from the built-in mic and not BlackHole.
- For each sentence the script shows the text. Press **Enter**, read it naturally, and press **Enter** again.
- In `--free` mode, talk freely for about 5–10 seconds per clip (about your day, for example). These clips are for telling speakers apart.
- Leave about half a second of silence before and after speaking, and don't cut off the last word.
- Recordings go to `eval/data/`, which is never uploaded to GitHub.

**3. Finish the model download** if you haven't yet: `python scripts/download_models.py --asr all`

**4. Run the benchmarks.** Each takes a few minutes, and the full `buzz` run will be the slowest:
```bash
for m in turbo-hy buzz mms turbo-zeroshot; do python eval/run_asr.py --model $m; done
python eval/run_asr.py --summary
python eval/run_translate.py
python eval/run_translate.py --from-asr turbo-hy
python eval/run_speakers.py
```

**5. Paste me the output** of `--summary`, `run_translate.py` and `run_speakers.py`. From those numbers I'll pick the default speech-recognition model and the speaker threshold, and record the reasons in `DECISIONS.md`.

If the friend can't be there in person, they can't record remotely with this script yet. In that case I could add a way to use WAV files they record on their phone.

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 15:35:07

Your MacBook mic isn't broken. macOS is blocking the program from using it.

**Why I think so:** −90 dB is the meter's lowest value (`livetr/cli.py:68`), which means the program received pure digital silence. A working built-in mic always picks up some room noise, around −60 to −70 dB even when nobody talks. You get exact silence when the app running the program has no **microphone permission**. In that case macOS sends zeros instead of showing an error. The "History restored" line shows you're using VS Code's built-in terminal, so VS Code is the app that needs the permission.

**Fix:**
1. Open **System Settings → Privacy & Security → Microphone**.
2. Turn on **Visual Studio Code**.
3. **Quit VS Code completely** (Cmd+Q) and reopen it. The permission only applies after a restart.
4. Run it again:
   ```bash
   python -m livetr --meter --mic "MacBook"
   ```
   Keep talking for the whole 15 seconds the meter runs, because the last line shows the level at the moment it stops.

If VS Code isn't in that list, use the normal **Terminal** app instead. Run `cd` into the project folder, then `source .venv/bin/activate` and the same command. macOS should then ask "Terminal would like to access the microphone"; click **Allow**.

The same permission problem probably explains the External Microphone result, so it may also work once permission is granted.

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 15:36:02

You can use the stream, but only for some of the measurements. A YouTube live stream has no written transcript, so it can't measure transcription accuracy on its own. It is good for the other two things:

| What you want to measure | Can the stream do it? |
|---|---|
| **Latency** (≤ 5 s end of phrase → English line) | ✅ Yes, directly |
| **Speaker labels** (Speaker 1 / 2 / …) | ✅ Partly. Works well if it's a talk show or interview with several people |
| **Transcription accuracy** (error rate) | ⚠️ Only if someone types out exactly what was said in the clips |
| **Translation quality** | ⚠️ Same; needs a correct English reference |

**1. Live test (no recording needed).** All the models you need are already downloaded, and BlackHole works (your meter showed −14 dB). Play the stream in your browser with the output set to Multi-Output Device, then run:
```bash
source .venv/bin/activate
python -m livetr --no-mic
```
`--no-mic` skips the microphone, so the mic permission problem doesn't matter for this test. Let it run 3–5 minutes, press **Ctrl-C**, and paste me the printed lines plus the **statistics at the end**: latency p50/p90/max and time per stage. Those latency numbers are the most important measurement for the 5 s requirement.

**2. Turn stream clips into eval data.** The recording script can record from BlackHole instead of the mic:
```bash
python eval/record.py --speaker stream1 --mic "BlackHole" --free 10
```
Press Enter to start and stop each clip, and catch one person speaking one or two sentences at a time. The clips are saved to `eval/data/` and never uploaded to GitHub. To use them for accuracy, a native speaker writes down the exact Armenian text of each clip, and I add it to the manifest.

Stream clips have one limit for speaker tests: you have to label each clip by who is speaking yourself, and a news stream mixes many voices. So the clips recorded with your friend (with a mic) are still the cleaner speaker data. The stream clips are a good extra test because they are real broadcast audio.

I'd start with step 1 and paste me the output. If the stream has music or background sound, expect some invented text during those parts. That's useful to see too.

"""Helpers shared by the evaluation scripts."""
from __future__ import annotations

import csv
import resource
import sys
import wave
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
DATA = ROOT / "eval" / "data"
RESULTS = ROOT / "eval" / "results"


def load_manifest(path: Path = DATA / "manifest.tsv") -> list[dict]:
    if not path.exists():
        raise SystemExit(f"{path} not found. Record clips first: python eval/record.py --speaker <name>")
    with open(path, encoding="utf-8") as f:
        return list(csv.DictReader(f, delimiter="\t"))


def load_wav(path: Path) -> np.ndarray:
    with wave.open(str(path), "rb") as w:
        if w.getframerate() != 16000 or w.getnchannels() != 1 or w.getsampwidth() != 2:
            raise ValueError(f"{path}: expected 16 kHz mono 16-bit WAV")
        pcm = np.frombuffer(w.readframes(w.getnframes()), dtype=np.int16)
    return pcm.astype(np.float32) / 32768.0


def peak_rss_gb() -> float:
    rss = resource.getrusage(resource.RUSAGE_SELF).ru_maxrss
    return rss / 1e9 if sys.platform == "darwin" else rss * 1024 / 1e9  # bytes on macOS, KiB on Linux
        w.writeframes(pcm.tobytes())


def main() -> None:
    p = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    p.add_argument("--speaker", required=True, help="short name of the person speaking, e.g. anna")
    p.add_argument("--mic", default=None, help="input device index or name (default: system default)")
    p.add_argument("--free", type=int, default=0, help="record N free-speech clips instead of the sentence list")
    args = p.parse_args()

    devices = dev.query_devices()
    idx = dev.resolve_input_device(args.mic, devices) if args.mic else dev.default_input_index()
    src = DeviceSource("mic", idx)
    print(f"recording from {src.describe()}")
    src.start()

    if args.free:
        items = [(f"free{i + 1:02d}", "", "Say anything for 3–6 seconds in Armenian.") for i in range(args.free)]
    else:
        with open(ROOT / "eval" / "sentences.tsv", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            items = [(r["id"], r["hy"], r["hy"]) for r in csv.DictReader(f, delimiter="\t")]

    manifest = DATA / "manifest.tsv"
    new_file = not manifest.exists()
    DATA.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    with open(manifest, "a", encoding="utf-8", newline="") as mf:
        w = csv.writer(mf, delimiter="\t")
        if new_file:
            w.writerow(["path", "speaker", "reference"])
        try:
            for clip_id, ref, prompt in items:
                print(f"\n[{clip_id}] {prompt}")
                audio = record_one(src)
                if len(audio) < 8000:
                    print("  too short, skipped")
                    continue
                rel = Path(args.speaker) / f"{clip_id}.wav"
                save_wav(DATA / rel, audio)
                w.writerow([str(rel), args.speaker, ref])
                mf.flush()
                print(f"  saved {rel} ({len(audio) / 16000:.1f}s)")
        finally:
            src.stop()


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
"""Benchmark one ASR model on the recorded clips: CER, WER, latency, memory.

    python eval/run_asr.py --model turbo-hy
    for m in buzz turbo-hy mms turbo-zeroshot; do python eval/run_asr.py --model $m; done
    python eval/run_asr.py --summary

One model per process so peak memory is measured cleanly. Each run appends one row to
eval/results/asr_results.csv and writes per-clip output to eval/results/asr_<model>.tsv.
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import csv
import os
import sys
import time

import numpy as np

from common import DATA, RESULTS, ROOT, load_manifest, load_wav, peak_rss_gb

sys.path.insert(0, str(ROOT))
from livetr.asr import MODELS, load_asr, pick_device  # noqa: E402
from livetr.metrics import cer_counts, wer_counts  # noqa: E402

SUMMARY = RESULTS / "asr_results.csv"
FIELDS = ["model", "device", "dtype", "clips", "cer", "wer", "lat_mean", "lat_p90", "lat_max", "rtf", "peak_rss_gb",
          "load_s", "date"]


def run(model: str, device: str, dtype: str) -> None:
    os.environ.setdefault("HF_HUB_OFFLINE", "1")
    rows = [r for r in load_manifest() if r["reference"].strip()]
    if not rows:
        raise SystemExit("no clips with a reference transcript in the manifest")

    t0 = time.monotonic()
    asr = load_asr(model, pick_device(device), dtype)
    load_s = time.monotonic() - t0
    asr.transcribe(np.zeros(16000, dtype=np.float32))  # warm-up, not measured

    RESULTS.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    c_err = c_tot = w_err = w_tot = 0
    lats, audio_s = [], 0.0
    with open(RESULTS / f"asr_{model}.tsv", "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="") as f:
        out = csv.writer(f, delimiter="\t")
        out.writerow(["path", "seconds", "latency", "cer", "reference", "hypothesis"])
        for r in rows:
            audio = load_wav(DATA / r["path"])
            t = time.monotonic()
            hyp = asr.transcribe(audio)
            lat = time.monotonic() - t
            ce, ct = cer_counts(r["reference"], hyp)
            we, wt = wer_counts(r["reference"], hyp)
            c_err, c_tot, w_err, w_tot = c_err + ce, c_tot + ct, w_err + we, w_tot + wt
            lats.append(lat)
            audio_s += len(audio) / 16000
            out.writerow([r["path"], f"{len(audio) / 16000:.2f}", f"{lat:.2f}", f"{ce / max(ct, 1):.3f}",
                          r["reference"], hyp])
            print(f"{lat:5.2f}s  CER {ce / max(ct, 1):5.1%}  {hyp}")
"""Download every model once, so the program can run fully offline.

    python scripts/download_models.py                 # default ASR + NLLB + speaker + VAD
    python scripts/download_models.py --asr buzz mms  # extra ASR candidates for benchmarking
    python scripts/download_models.py --asr all
"""
from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import sys
from pathlib import Path

sys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent))

from livetr.asr import MODELS  # noqa: E402
from livetr.cli import DEFAULT_ASR  # noqa: E402

NLLB_REPO = "facebook/nllb-200-distilled-600M"


def main() -> None:
    p = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    p.add_argument("--asr", nargs="+", default=[DEFAULT_ASR], help=f"keys from {list(MODELS)} or 'all'")
    p.add_argument("--nllb-dir", default="models/nllb-200-distilled-600M-ct2-int8")
    args = p.parse_args()
    keys = list(MODELS) if args.asr == ["all"] else args.asr

    from huggingface_hub import snapshot_download

    for key in keys:
        spec = MODELS[key]
        print(f"[asr] {key}: {spec.repo}")
        # MMS ships ~1,100 language adapters; fetch only the shared weights and the Armenian one.
        allow = None if spec.kind == "whisper" else ["*.json", "*.txt", "model*.safetensors", f"adapter.{spec.language}.*"]
        snapshot_download(spec.repo, allow_patterns=allow)

    out = Path(args.nllb_dir)
    if (out / "model.bin").exists():
        print(f"[mt] {out} already exists")
    else:
        import ctranslate2
        from transformers import AutoTokenizer

        print(f"[mt] converting {NLLB_REPO} to CTranslate2 int8 -> {out}")
        ctranslate2.converters.TransformersConverter(NLLB_REPO).convert(str(out), quantization="int8")
        AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(NLLB_REPO).save_pretrained(str(out))

    print("[spk] speechbrain/spkrec-ecapa-voxceleb")
    from livetr.speakers import EcapaEmbedder

    EcapaEmbedder()

    print("[vad] silero (ships inside the pip package)")
    from livetr.segmenter import SileroVAD

    SileroVAD()
    print("done. The program now runs offline.")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

"""CER / WER for Armenian, with a fixed normalisation so numbers are comparable between models."""
from __future__ import annotations

import re
import unicodedata

# Armenian stress/exclamation/question marks (՚ ՛ ՜ ՞ ՟) are written INSIDE the word (ինչպե՞ս): delete them.
_IN_WORD = re.compile(r"[՚-՜՞՟]")
# Separating punctuation: ՝ (comma) ։ (full stop) ֊ (hyphen) « » and ASCII punctuation: replace with a space.
_PUNCT = re.compile(r"[՝։֊«»…\"'`.,!?;:()\[\]{}\-–—/\\]")
_SPACE = re.compile(r"\s+")


def normalize_hy(text: str) -> str:
    text = unicodedata.normalize("NFC", text).lower()
    text = text.replace("և", "եւ")  # the ligature and the two letters are the same word
    text = _IN_WORD.sub("", text)
    text = _PUNCT.sub(" ", text)
    return _SPACE.sub(" ", text).strip()


def edit_distance(ref: list, hyp: list) -> int:
    prev = list(range(len(hyp) + 1))
    for i, r in enumerate(ref, 1):
        cur = [i] + [0] * len(hyp)
        for j, h in enumerate(hyp, 1):
            cur[j] = min(prev[j] + 1, cur[j - 1] + 1, prev[j - 1] + (r != h))
        prev = cur
    return prev[-1]


def wer_counts(ref: str, hyp: str) -> tuple[int, int]:
    """(errors, reference words) after normalisation."""
    r, h = normalize_hy(ref).split(), normalize_hy(hyp).split()
    return edit_distance(r, h), len(r)


def cer_counts(ref: str, hyp: str) -> tuple[int, int]:
    """(errors, reference characters) after normalisation; spaces are ignored."""
    r, h = list(normalize_hy(ref).replace(" ", "")), list(normalize_hy(hyp).replace(" ", ""))
```

README.md
# Benchmark results

Written by `eval/run_asr.py` (and committed, because they are the evidence for the model choice):
- `asr_results.csv`: one row per model run: CER, WER, latency mean/p90/max, real-time factor, peak memory.
- `asr_<model>.tsv`: per-clip reference vs hypothesis, to see *what kind* of errors a model makes.

No audio is stored here.
centroid. They get a known label only if similarity ≥ `strong_threshold` (0.65), otherwise `Speaker ?`.
Why: an honest "unknown" is better than a confident wrong label (brief: honesty about limits beats hiding them).
Also: if the speaker limit (8) is reached, an unmatched segment is `Speaker ?` rather than forced into a cluster.

## 2026-10-21 — ASR candidates (decision pending measurement)
Benchmark on our own recordings with `eval/run_asr.py`:
1. `BuzzASR/armenian` (Whisper large-v3 fine-tune; card: FLEURS WER 11.9 %, CV WER 2.4 %). ~3 GB fp16.
2. `Chillarmo/whisper-large-v3-turbo-armenian` (turbo fine-tune, ~1.6 GB fp16, faster decoder). No trusted WER.
3. `facebook/mms-1b-all` with `hye` adapter (CTC baseline).
4. `openai/whisper-large-v3-turbo` zero-shot (reference: shows why a fine-tune is needed).
Runtime: Hugging Face transformers on MPS, fp16. Not mlx-whisper, because BuzzASR replaced the tokenizer.
**Provisional default: `turbo-hy`** (fits memory comfortably). Replace with the measured winner.

## 2026-10-21 — Translation: NLLB-200 distilled 600M, CTranslate2 int8, CPU
- Supports `hye_Armn → eng_Latn`, small, fast on CPU, leaves MPS free for ASR.
- Alternative: NLLB 1.3B distilled if quality is poor and memory allows (measure first).
- License CC-BY-NC 4.0: fine for this challenge, noted in README.

## 2026-10-21 — Resampling with soxr streaming resampler
Devices run at 44.1/48 kHz; models need 16 kHz. soxr's `ResampleStream` resamples block by block without edge
artifacts (plain per-block `resample_poly` clicks at block boundaries).

## 2026-10-21 — Python 3.11
Widest wheel support for torch / ctranslate2 / speechbrain on macOS arm64.

def main() -> None:
    os.environ.setdefault("HF_HUB_OFFLINE", "1")
    p = argparse.ArgumentParser()
    p.add_argument("--nllb-dir", default=str(ROOT / "models/nllb-200-distilled-600M-ct2-int8"))
    p.add_argument("--from-asr", default=None, help="use eval/results/asr_<model>.tsv hypotheses as input")
    a = p.parse_args()

    if a.from_asr:
        with open(RESULTS / f"asr_{a.from_asr}.tsv", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            items = [(r["hypothesis"], "") for r in csv.DictReader(f, delimiter="\t")]
    else:
        with open(ROOT / "eval/sentences.tsv", encoding="utf-8") as f:
            items = [(r["hy"], r["en"]) for r in csv.DictReader(f, delimiter="\t")]

    tr = NllbTranslator(a.nllb_dir)
    tr.translate("Բարև")  # warm-up
    lats = []
    for hy, ref in items:
        t = time.monotonic()
        en = tr.translate(hy)
        lats.append(time.monotonic() - t)
        print(f"HY : {hy}\nMT : {en}" + (f"\nREF: {ref}" if ref else "") + f"\n     ({lats[-1]:.2f}s)\n")
    print(f"mean {sum(lats) / len(lats):.2f}s  max {max(lats):.2f}s over {len(lats)} sentences")


if __name__ == "__main__":

| `mms` | `facebook/mms-1b-all` + Armenian adapter | CTC: fast, cannot loop or hallucinate long text | usually less accurate, no punctuation |
| `turbo-zeroshot` | `openai/whisper-large-v3-turbo` | reference point | expected to be clearly worse |

Rejected without benchmarking: small/tiny Armenian Whisper fine-tunes (published WER 40–78 %); cloud ASR
(not allowed); mlx-whisper (faster on Apple Silicon, but BuzzASR replaced Whisper's tokenizer, which
mlx-whisper does not support. Worth revisiting for `turbo-hy`).

Default is `turbo-hy` **provisionally** (fits in memory with room to spare). The final choice is the model with
the lowest CER whose p90 latency on our clips is ≤ ~2.5 s and that does not push the Mac into swap.
Results: `eval/results/asr_results.csv`.

### Translation: NLLB-200 distilled 600M, CTranslate2 int8, on CPU
Supports `hye_Armn → eng_Latn`, about 0.6 GB in int8, typically well under a second per sentence on CPU,
and it leaves the GPU to ASR. The 1.3B version is better but needs roughly twice the memory. It is the first
upgrade to try if translations are poor and memory allows. LLM-based translators were not considered:
too large for 8 GB next to Whisper. Licence of NLLB weights: CC-BY-NC 4.0 (fine for this challenge, not for commercial use).

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 15:46:00

I committed the FLEURS step locally (`8c53486`) but haven't pushed it. Audio stays local; only the scripts and result tables are in the commit.

Yes, FLEURS only covers transcription accuracy. Speaker separation and latency need their own tests, and the PDF you're pointing to shows how to do both **without friends**:

| Part | What the PDF did | How we'd do the same |
|---|---|---|
| **Speaker threshold** | Compared voice-print similarity for pairs of FLEURS recordings of the *same sentence*. Different readers scored −0.1…0.4 and the same reader twice scored 0.6…1.0, so the threshold went in the gap (0.40). | A new `eval/fleurs_pairs.py` that computes the same histogram on FLEURS test and picks our threshold from the gap. Our current 0.45 is still a guess. |
| **Speaker separation** | One test call with 3 speakers and 18 turns: all labelled right, and still right after 16 kbps Opus, the codec video calls use. | Build a test-call WAV from FLEURS clips of 3 speakers, plus an Opus-compressed copy made with `ffmpeg`, which you have. Replay both with `python -m livetr --file`, which already exists, and count correct labels. |
| **Latency** | A per-stage breakdown against the 5 s limit, from replayed and live runs. | Comes free from the same `--file` replay, which runs in real time and prints p50/p90/max and time per stage at the end. |

**One limit:** FLEURS doesn't say who the reader is. For the test call, I'd take the "same reader" groups that the similarity histogram finds, which seems to be what the PDF did, and note that honestly in DECISIONS.md.

**What the first number already shows:** ASR alone takes about 2.45 s per phrase. Add 0.5 s of silence detection plus translation and we're around 3.5 s, under 5 s but not by much. The PDF reached 0.7 s with an 8-bit MLX version and a 12 s window. That's a later improvement, after measuring the other models.

None of this replaces **one real practice call** before the interview, since the interview itself is a live call with 2+ people.

Should I build the threshold histogram first, then the synthetic test call, as two separate commits? And should I push `8c53486` to GitHub now or wait?

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 15:48:37

Yes. A YouTube video with 2–3 Armenian speakers, played through BlackHole, reaches the program exactly the way people on a call would. You don't need friends for practice runs.

**Pick videos like these:**
- ✅ Interviews, podcasts and talk shows with **2–3 people taking turns**. Mixed male/female voices are easiest; two similar voices are the harder, more realistic test.
- ✅ Clear speech, little background music, people mostly not talking over each other
- ❌ News with music under the voice, or TikTok and Reels: usually one speaker, music and very short clips. That tests noise handling, not speaker separation.
- ❌ Western Armenian or heavy slang, at least for the first tests. The models are weaker there; that's a good later test for the README limitations.

**Option 1: play it live (quickest).** Play the video in the browser, with output set to Multi-Output Device and headphones on:
```bash
python -m livetr --no-mic
```
After 3–5 minutes press Ctrl-C. The latency statistics print automatically. For speaker separation, check while listening whether each person keeps the same `Speaker N` label.

**Option 2: record it once, replay as often as you like (better for measuring).**
```bash
ffmpeg -f avfoundation -i ":BlackHole 2ch" -t 300 -ac 1 -ar 16000 eval/data/yt_call1.wav
python -m livetr --file eval/data/yt_call1.wav
```
The first command records 5 minutes of whatever plays. The replay runs in real time, so the latency numbers are realistic. Because the audio is identical every time, you can compare settings fairly, for example `--speaker-threshold 0.40` against `0.45`. Write down who speaks when (e.g. "0:00 host, 0:12 guest, …") once, and you can count correct labels. The file stays in `eval/data/` and is never uploaded.

**Option 3: closest to the interview.** Start a Google Meet or Zoom call between your laptop and your phone, play the YouTube video on the phone, and let the call carry it to the laptop. Then the audio passes through the call app's compression and noise filtering, just like in the interview.

**What videos can't give you:** an error rate for transcription, because there's no exact reference text. FLEURS already covers that, so the two together give you all three measurements.

Do one real call before the interview anyway, at least with Option 3. Also add the `Me` side: speak yourself between the video's turns, once the mic permission for VS Code or Terminal is fixed.

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 15:53:42

There are two parts here: saving the run's output so I can analyse it, and getting this chat into `chats/` for the submission.

**1. Save the Option 1 output**

The program only prints to the terminal; saving transcripts is out of scope in the brief. You can still capture the terminal with `tee`:
```bash
python -m livetr --no-mic --no-color 2>&1 | tee -i eval/data/live_yt1.txt
```
- `--no-color` keeps the file free of colour codes.
- `tee -i` keeps writing when you press Ctrl-C, so the **final statistics** (latency p50/p90/max, time per stage) end up in the file too. Without `-i` they would be lost.
- `eval/data/` is git-ignored, so the transcript of someone else's video isn't uploaded.

Then just tell me "read eval/data/live_yt1.txt". I'll check the speaker labels and latency and record the numbers in `DECISIONS.md`, and those go to GitHub as your evidence. Also tell me the video link and roughly who speaks when (e.g. "2 people, host male, guest female"), so I can judge whether the labels are right.

**2. Put this chat into `chats/`**

Type this in the Claude Code chat box:
```
/export chats/02-setup-and-eval.md
```
That saves this whole session as Markdown. If your version only offers "copy to clipboard", paste it into a new file `chats/02-setup-and-eval.md`. Export the earlier session too (the one that wrote the plan and code, linked in the first commit) as `01-plan-and-skeleton.md`. Do this at the end, once you've finished working in each session, so the export is complete.

**One thing to decide before you submit.** `chats/README.md` says "Every Claude **Cowork** session", but these sessions are Claude Code. Either change that line to say Claude Code, or ask Sven whether that's acceptable. His email says questions are welcome, and the brief says "Using any other AI assistant is not allowed". It's better to be upfront than to have them find it in the interview.

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 15:55:59

No, there's no minimum. Neither PDF sets a duration; the brief only says the interview will be *"a short live conversation."* The 3–5 minutes was my suggestion.

What matters is how many **turns** the run contains, not the clock:
- **Speaker separation:** aim for about 15–20 turns in total, with each person speaking at least 4–5 times. The program has to recognise the same voice again several times before you can say "labels stay stable." The PDF's test call had 18 turns.
- **Latency:** every phrase gives one measurement, so 15–20 phrases make p50/p90 meaningful. With 5 phrases a single slow one decides the result.

In a lively interview video, 2–3 minutes can already give you that. In a slow one with long monologues it may take 5+ minutes. So run until you have about 15–20 turns, then press Ctrl-C.

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 15:56:28

The eval collection has no required duration either. Neither the brief nor the tips set one; the tips only say *"a few short Armenian recordings with the correct text."* So you decide, as long as the numbers are meaningful.

For each part, here's what's enough:

| Eval part | Source | Enough | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Transcription accuracy | FLEURS | 40 clips (≈5 min of audio) | ✅ done |
| Speaker threshold | FLEURS same-sentence pairs | ~200+ pairs (the PDF used about that) | not built yet |
| Speaker separation | YouTube video with 2–3 people | ~15–20 turns, each person 4+ times | you |
| Latency | the same YouTube run | ~15–20 phrases | you (same run) |

For the YouTube part that means one video, about 2–5 minutes depending on how often people switch. If you want stronger evidence, two different videos are better than one long one, for example one with a male and a female voice and one with two similar voices. That shows where speaker separation works and where it fails, which the brief values: *"honest about its limits."*

        return requested
    return "mps" if torch.backends.mps.is_available() else "cpu"


class WhisperASR:
    def __init__(self, repo: str, language: str | None, device: str, dtype: str = "float16"):
        import torch
        from transformers import WhisperForConditionalGeneration, WhisperProcessor

        self._torch = torch
        self.device = device
        self.dtype = getattr(torch, dtype)
        self.language = language
        self.processor = WhisperProcessor.from_pretrained(repo)
        self.model = WhisperForConditionalGeneration.from_pretrained(repo, dtype=self.dtype).to(device).eval()

    def transcribe(self, audio: np.ndarray) -> str:
        feats = self.processor(audio, sampling_rate=SAMPLE_RATE, return_tensors="pt").input_features
        feats = feats.to(self.device, self.dtype)
        kwargs = dict(
            num_beams=1,                 # greedy: beam search costs latency we don't have
            no_repeat_ngram_size=3,      # stops the classic Whisper repetition loop
            max_new_tokens=160,          # 8 s of speech never needs more
        )
        if self.language is not None:
            kwargs.update(language=self.language, task="transcribe")
        with self._torch.no_grad():
            ids = self.model.generate(feats, **kwargs)
        return self.processor.batch_decode(ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0].strip()


class MmsASR:
    def __init__(self, repo: str, adapter: str, device: str, dtype: str = "float16"):
        import torch
        from transformers import AutoProcessor, Wav2Vec2ForCTC

        self._torch = torch
        self.device = device
        self.dtype = getattr(torch, dtype)
        self.processor = AutoProcessor.from_pretrained(repo)
        self.processor.tokenizer.set_target_lang(adapter)
        model = Wav2Vec2ForCTC.from_pretrained(repo)
        model.load_adapter(adapter)
        self.model = model.to(device, self.dtype).eval()

    def transcribe(self, audio: np.ndarray) -> str:
        inputs = self.processor(audio, sampling_rate=SAMPLE_RATE, return_tensors="pt")
        values = inputs.input_values.to(self.device, self.dtype)
        with self._torch.no_grad():
            logits = self.model(values).logits
        ids = logits.argmax(dim=-1)[0]
        return self.processor.decode(ids).strip()


def load_asr(key: str, device: str, dtype: str = "float16"):
    if key not in MODELS:
"""Armenian text -> English text with NLLB-200 (CTranslate2, int8, CPU)."""
from __future__ import annotations

from pathlib import Path

DEFAULT_MODEL_DIR = "models/nllb-200-distilled-600M-ct2-int8"


class NllbTranslator:
    def __init__(
        self,
        model_dir: str = DEFAULT_MODEL_DIR,
        src_lang: str = "hye_Armn",
        tgt_lang: str = "eng_Latn",
        threads: int = 4,
        beam_size: int = 2,
    ):
        if not (Path(model_dir) / "model.bin").exists():
            raise FileNotFoundError(
                f"No converted NLLB model in '{model_dir}'. Run: python scripts/download_models.py"
            )
        import ctranslate2
        from transformers import AutoTokenizer

        self.tgt_lang = tgt_lang
        self.beam_size = beam_size
        self.tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_dir, src_lang=src_lang)
        self.translator = ctranslate2.Translator(
            model_dir, device="cpu", compute_type="int8", intra_threads=threads
        )

    def translate(self, text: str) -> str:
        tokens = self.tokenizer.convert_ids_to_tokens(self.tokenizer.encode(text))
        result = self.translator.translate_batch(
            [tokens],
            target_prefix=[[self.tgt_lang]],
            beam_size=self.beam_size,
            max_decoding_length=256,
        )
        out_tokens = result[0].hypotheses[0][1:]  # drop the target-language token
        return self.tokenizer.decode(
            self.tokenizer.convert_tokens_to_ids(out_tokens), skip_special_tokens=True
        ).strip()
```

# Tests and QA checklist

Derived from the brief's requirements, not from the code. Tick items by hand before the interview.
Automated tests: `pytest` (pure logic only, no models or audio devices needed).

## R1 — Two audio sources at once
- [ ] `python -m livetr --list-devices` shows the mic and `BlackHole 2ch`.
- [ ] `python -m livetr --meter` shows level bars moving for mic when I talk and for system when a video plays.
- [ ] I still hear the call while BlackHole captures it (Multi-Output Device selected as output).
- [ ] Wrong device name → program stops with a clear error listing the input devices. (automated: `test_devices.py`)
- [ ] Ambiguous device name (matches 2 devices) → clear error. (automated)

## R2 — Armenian transcription
- [ ] CER/WER measured for every candidate on our own recordings (`eval/results/asr_results.csv`).
- [ ] Silence and background noise do not produce invented text (5 min idle test: count printed lines).
- [ ] Numbers, names (Երևան, Գյումրի) and questions (՞) come out readable.
- [ ] CER/WER metric is correct on known examples. (automated: `test_metrics.py`)

## R3 — English translation
- [ ] `eval/run_translate.py` on the sentence list: every line understandable (judged by an Armenian speaker).
- [ ] Empty ASR result → no translation call, nothing printed. (automated: `test_pipeline.py`)

## R4 — Speaker identification
- [ ] Mic segments always labelled `Me`. (automated)
- [ ] Two different call-side voices get different labels. (automated with synthetic embeddings; manual on recordings)
- [ ] Same voice keeps its label for the whole session (10+ minutes). (automated: stable centroid test)
- [ ] Short segments do not create new speakers. (automated)
- [ ] `eval/run_speakers.py` similarity distributions: same-speaker vs different-speaker separate cleanly?
- [ ] Two call participants of the same gender / similar voice: are they kept apart? (known hard case)

## R5 — Live output
- [ ] Each line shows time, speaker, HY, EN. (automated: `test_output.py`)
- [ ] Lines appear while the conversation is running, not at the end.

## C1 — Latency ≤ 5 s
- [ ] `--stats` summary at exit: p50/p90/max latency per source under 5 s.
- [ ] Long monologue (> 8 s) is force-cut and each part arrives in time. (automated: segmenter force-cut test)
- [ ] Both sides talking at once: queue wait visible, latency still acceptable?
- [ ] First segment after start is not slow (warm-up done at startup).
- [ ] Memory: Activity Monitor shows no swap during a 10-minute call.

## C2 — Local only
- [ ] Turn Wi-Fi off after models are downloaded → program still runs (`HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1`).

## Interview rehearsal
- [ ] Real call (Zoom/Meet) with 2 people on the call side, 5 minutes, Armenian.
- [ ] Start-up time from command to "listening" noted.
- [ ] Chat transcripts exported to `chats/`, one .md per session.
5:## 2026-10-21 — Target hardware: Apple Silicon, 8 GB RAM
9:## 2026-10-21 — Pipeline shape: VAD-segmented utterances, one shared processing worker
16:## 2026-10-21 — Segment end = 500 ms of silence, force-cut at 8 s, drop < 0.4 s of speech
22:## 2026-10-21 — `Me` = everything from the microphone; no speaker model on the mic side
25:## 2026-10-21 — Call-side speakers: ECAPA embeddings + online centroid clustering
33:## 2026-10-21 — Short call-side segments are labelled `Speaker ?` unless very close to a known speaker
39:## 2026-10-21 — ASR candidates (decision pending measurement)
48:## 2026-10-21 — Translation: NLLB-200 distilled 600M, CTranslate2 int8, CPU
53:## 2026-10-21 — Resampling with soxr streaming resampler
57:## 2026-10-21 — Python 3.11
60:## 2026-10-23 — ASR test set from Google FLEURS (hy_am, test split)
```

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 16:06:48

Steps 1 and 2 are committed (`11e2a59`, `58f023c`). The model comparison is still running, and the second video is your part. Nothing is pushed to GitHub yet.

**Step 1: the warnings**
- **Per-phrase warning:** fixed. Fixing it uncovered a real bug: the transcription limit of 160 tokens was too short for Armenian, which needs about 15–21 tokens per second of speech. It cut off 4 of the 40 FLEURS clips in the middle of a letter, and fast speakers in long live phrases likely got cut too. I raised the limit to 224. Re-measured with turbo-hy: errors dropped from 13.1 % to 12.6 % CER, at the same speed.
- **Translator tokenizer warning:** a false alarm. Our tokenizer matches the original NLLB model on 65 of 66 sentences, and the one difference (`«`) behaves the same in both. I left the warning visible and documented it. Side finding: NLLB doesn't know `«»` at all, which is listed in TESTS.md as something to measure.

**Step 2: docs.** `DECISIONS.md` now has the live-test numbers, the token-limit fix and the tokenizer check. `TESTS.md` ticks what's verified and adds new checks.

**Step 3: in progress**
- **Model comparison:** `turbo-zeroshot`, `mms` and `buzz` are running on the 40 FLEURS clips, one after another because of the 8 GB RAM. `buzz` is the largest and slowest. I'll post the comparison table when they finish.
- **Second video (yours):** pick one with lots of back-and-forth between 2–3 people, then run:
  ```bash
  python -m livetr --no-mic --no-color 2>&1 | tee -i eval/data/live_yt2.txt

108	
109	## 4. Models and why
110	
111	Target machine: MacBook Apple Silicon with **8 GB RAM**. Memory is the main constraint, then the 5 s budget.
112	
113	### ASR (Armenian speech → text): decision by measurement
114	Zero-shot Whisper is weak on Armenian, so only Armenian fine-tunes are serious candidates.
115	
116	| key | model | why consider it | risk |
117	|---|---|---|---|
118	| `buzz` | `BuzzASR/armenian` (Whisper large-v3 full fine-tune) | best published numbers among open models (card: FLEURS WER 11.9 %, Common Voice WER 2.4 %) | ~3 GB in fp16 on an 8 GB Mac; large-v3 decoder is slower |
119	| `turbo-hy` | `Chillarmo/whisper-large-v3-turbo-armenian` | turbo has 4 decoder layers → much faster, ~1.6 GB | no trustworthy published WER, must measure |
120	| `mms` | `facebook/mms-1b-all` + Armenian adapter | CTC: fast, cannot loop or hallucinate long text | usually less accurate, no punctuation |
121	| `turbo-zeroshot` | `openai/whisper-large-v3-turbo` | reference point | expected to be clearly worse |
122	
123	Rejected without benchmarking: small/tiny Armenian Whisper fine-tunes (published WER 40–78 %); cloud ASR
124	(not allowed); mlx-whisper (faster on Apple Silicon, but BuzzASR replaced Whisper's tokenizer, which
125	mlx-whisper does not support. Worth revisiting for `turbo-hy`).
126	
127	Default is `turbo-hy` **provisionally** (fits in memory with room to spare). The final choice is the model with
128	the lowest CER whose p90 latency on our clips is ≤ ~2.5 s and that does not push the Mac into swap.
129	Results: `eval/results/asr_results.csv`.

I tested converting them before translation on all 33 FLEURS sentences that contain them, scored against the official English: no difference (chrF 60.8 both ways, 16 sentences better, 15 worse, one sentence dropped entirely). Since it doesn't help, I didn't add the code, and the result is in `DECISIONS.md`.

**Default model, `buzz`, the most accurate:**

| | buzz | previous turbo-hy |
|---|---|---|
| Character errors | **4.6 %** | 12.6 % |
| Word errors | **11.6 %** | 45.6 % |
| ASR time p90 | 2.98 s | 2.73 s |
| GPU memory | 3.5 GB | 2.2 GB |

Text quality should be far better: word errors drop from about 1 in 2 to about 1 in 9. The cost is speed and memory. In live test 1, turbo-hy already reached 4.69 s, and buzz is about 0.3 s slower per phrase, so **buzz may go over 5 s on long phrases**. README and DECISIONS say this openly, with `--asr mms` as the fallback: almost as accurate per character, and 4–5× faster.

**Your second video now does two jobs:** it tests speaker switches, and it's the live check for buzz.
```bash
python -m livetr --no-mic --no-color 2>&1 | tee -i eval/data/live_yt2.txt
```
Pick a video with lots of back-and-forth between 2–3 people, stop after 10+ switches, and tell me to check it. If the latency goes over 5 s there, we switch to `mms` and test that live too.

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 21:21:19

Among the models we measured, **buzz is the best for Armenian.** Here they are in plain words, best first, with numbers from our test on 40 Armenian FLEURS clips:

| Rank | Name in the program | Real model | What it is | Word errors | Speed per phrase |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 🥇 | `buzz` | **BuzzASR/armenian** | OpenAI's biggest Whisper (large-v3), retrained fully on Armenian | **11.6 %**, about 1 wrong word in 9 | 2.8 s (slow) |
| 🥈 | `mms` | **facebook/mms-1b-all** (Armenian adapter) | Meta's model for 1,100+ languages, with an Armenian add-on | 21.9 %, about 1 in 5 | **0.6 s (very fast)** |
| 🥉 | `turbo-hy` | Chillarmo/whisper-large-v3-turbo-armenian | Smaller, faster Whisper "turbo", retrained on Armenian by one person | 45.6 %, about 1 in 2 | 2.4 s |
| 4 | `turbo-zeroshot` | openai/whisper-large-v3-turbo | Original Whisper, no Armenian training | 68.4 % | 2.4 s |

**Why turbo-hy was bad:** "turbo" is a cut-down Whisper with only 4 decoder layers instead of 32, so it's faster but less capable. Its Armenian retraining was also smaller. buzz keeps the full model and was trained more thoroughly.

**Which to use:**
- **buzz** if text quality matters most. It's now the default.
- **mms** if buzz turns out too slow live. Its letters are almost as accurate (5.2 % vs 4.6 % character errors), but it often splits or joins words wrongly and writes no punctuation (`,` `։` `՞`), which makes translation harder.

**One more strong candidate we haven't tested:** **NVIDIA `stt_hy_fastconformer_hybrid_large_pc`**, made specifically for Armenian. It's small (about 0.4 GB), likely very fast, and writes punctuation and capitals (the `pc` in its name), so it could combine mms's speed with better text. You already have it downloaded on your Mac from an earlier project. The catch: it needs NVIDIA's NeMo library, a large extra install. The results PDF you showed also listed it as "not benchmarked." I can add it to the comparison if you want; it would be one new candidate, measured the same way on the 40 FLEURS clips.

All these models are trained on **Eastern Armenian** (Armenia), and FLEURS is Eastern too. Western Armenian (diaspora) will come out worse with any of them, which belongs in the README limitations.

def load_asr(key: str, device: str, dtype: str = "float16"):
    if key not in MODELS:
        raise ValueError(f"Unknown ASR model '{key}'. Choose from: {', '.join(MODELS)}")
    spec = MODELS[key]
    if spec.kind == "whisper":
        return WhisperASR(spec.repo, spec.language, device, dtype)
    if spec.kind == "mms":
        return MmsASR(spec.repo, spec.language, device, dtype)
    raise ValueError(f"Unknown model kind {spec.kind}")

        with self._torch.no_grad():
            logits = self.model(values).logits
        ids = logits.argmax(dim=-1)[0]

class NemoASR:
    def __init__(self, repo: str, device: str):
        from pathlib import Path

        from huggingface_hub import snapshot_download
        from nemo.collections.asr.models import ASRModel

        files = sorted(Path(snapshot_download(repo, allow_patterns=["*.nemo"])).glob("*.nemo"))
        if len(files) != 1:
            raise RuntimeError(f"expected one .nemo file in {repo}, found {[f.name for f in files]}")
        self.model = ASRModel.restore_from(str(files[0]), map_location=device).eval()

    def transcribe(self, audio: np.ndarray) -> str:
        out = self.model.transcribe([audio], batch_size=1, verbose=False)
        return out[0].text.strip()


def load_asr(key: str, device: str, dtype: str = "float16"):
    if key not in MODELS:
        raise ValueError(f"Unknown ASR model '{key}'. Choose from: {', '.join(MODELS)}")
    spec = MODELS[key]
    if spec.kind == "whisper":
        return WhisperASR(spec.repo, spec.language, device, dtype)
    if spec.kind == "mms":
        return MmsASR(spec.repo, spec.language, device, dtype)
    if spec.kind == "nemo":
        return NemoASR(spec.repo, device)  # runs in float32; dtype applies to the torch/transformers models
    raise ValueError(f"Unknown model kind {spec.kind}")
"""Command line entry point: python -m livetr [options]"""
from __future__ import annotations

import argparse
import os
import sys
import time

import numpy as np

from livetr.asr import MODELS

DEFAULT_ASR = "buzz"  # most accurate on FLEURS; see DECISIONS.md (2026-10-23)


def build_parser() -> argparse.ArgumentParser:
    p = argparse.ArgumentParser(
        prog="python -m livetr",
        description="Live Armenian transcription + English translation with speaker labels (local models only).",
    )
    g = p.add_argument_group("audio")
    g.add_argument("--list-devices", action="store_true", help="print input devices and exit")
    g.add_argument("--meter", action="store_true", help="show input levels for both sources (setup check) and exit")
    g.add_argument("--mic", default=None, help="microphone: device index or part of its name (default: system default input)")
    g.add_argument("--system", default="BlackHole", help="system-audio loopback device (default: 'BlackHole')")
    g.add_argument("--no-mic", action="store_true", help="do not capture the microphone")
    g.add_argument("--no-system", action="store_true", help="do not capture system audio")
    g.add_argument("--file", default=None, help="play a 16-bit WAV file in real time instead of live devices")
    g.add_argument("--file-source", choices=["mic", "system"], default="system", help="treat --file as this source")

    m = p.add_argument_group("models")
    m.add_argument("--asr", choices=sorted(MODELS), default=DEFAULT_ASR, help=f"ASR model (default {DEFAULT_ASR})")
    m.add_argument("--device", choices=["auto", "mps", "cpu"], default="auto", help="device for ASR")
    m.add_argument("--asr-dtype", choices=["float16", "float32"], default="float16")
    m.add_argument("--nllb-dir", default="models/nllb-200-distilled-600M-ct2-int8")
    m.add_argument("--allow-download", action="store_true",
                   help="allow Hugging Face downloads at runtime (default: offline, models must be downloaded first)")

    s = p.add_argument_group("segmentation and speakers")
    s.add_argument("--vad-threshold", type=float, default=0.5)
    s.add_argument("--min-silence-ms", type=int, default=500)
    s.add_argument("--max-segment-s", type=float, default=8.0)
    s.add_argument("--speaker-threshold", type=float, default=0.45, help="cosine similarity for 'same speaker'")
    s.add_argument("--max-speakers", type=int, default=8)

    o = p.add_argument_group("output")
    o.add_argument("--no-color", action="store_true")
    o.add_argument("--quiet-timings", action="store_true", help="hide per-line latency details")
    return p

python -m livetr --list-devices          # find the indices / names
python -m livetr --meter                 # talk -> 'mic' bar moves; play a video -> 'system' bar moves
```

## 2. Run
```bash
python -m livetr                                   # default mic + "BlackHole" device
python -m livetr --mic "MacBook" --system 3        # pick devices by name part or index
python -m livetr --asr buzz                        # another ASR model
python -m livetr --file call.wav                   # replay a recording in real time (no devices needed)
python -m livetr --no-mic                          # only the call side
```
Ctrl-C stops and prints session statistics (latency p50/p90/max per source, time per stage, dropped segments).

Useful knobs: `--min-silence-ms` (pause that ends a phrase, default 500), `--max-segment-s` (forced cut,
default 8), `--speaker-threshold` (default 0.45), `--vad-threshold` (default 0.5). Run `--help` for all of them.
````

The Whisper and MMS candidates it was compared\nagainst were removed from the code afterwards; their numbers stay in DECISIONS.md and eval/results/.\n\"\"\"\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nfrom dataclasses import dataclass\n\nimport numpy as np\n\n\n@dataclass(frozen=True)\nclass AsrSpec:\n    repo: str\n    note: str\n\n\nMODELS: dict[str, AsrSpec] = {\n    \"nemo-hy\": AsrSpec(\n        \"nvidia/stt_hy_fastconformer_hybrid_large_pc\",\n        \"NVIDIA FastConformer hybrid RNNT/CTC (~115M) trained for Armenian, with punctuation and capitals\",\n    ),\n}\n\n\ndef pick_device(requested: str = \"auto\") -> str:\n    import torch\n\n    if requested != \"auto\":\n        if requested == \"mps\" and not torch.backends.mps.is_available():\n            raise RuntimeError(\"--device mps requested but MPS is not available on this machine.\")\n        return requested\n    return \"mps\" if torch.backends.mps.is_available() else \"cpu\"\n\n\nclass NemoASR:\n    def __init__(self, repo: str, device: str):\n        from pathlib import Path\n\n        from huggingface_hub import snapshot_download\n        from nemo.collections.asr.models import ASRModel\n\n        files = sorted(Path(snapshot_download(repo, allow_patterns=[\"*.nemo\"])).glob(\"*.nemo\"))\n        if len(files) != 1:\n            raise RuntimeError(f\"expected one .nemo file in {repo}, found {[f.name for f in files]}\")\n        self.model = ASRModel.restore_from(str(files[0]), map_location=device).eval()\n\n    def transcribe(self, audio: np.ndarray) -> str:\n        out = self.model.transcribe([audio], batch_size=1, verbose=False)\n        return out[0].text.strip()\n\n\ndef load_asr(key: str, device: str) -> NemoASR:\n    if key not in MODELS:\n        raise ValueError(f\"Unknown ASR model '{key}'. Choose from: {', '.join(MODELS)}\")\n    return NemoASR(MODELS[key].repo, device)\n"
}

**Tool result:**

```
### ASR (Armenian speech → text): decision by measurement
Zero-shot Whisper is weak on Armenian, so only Armenian fine-tunes are serious candidates.

| key | model | why consider it | risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| `buzz` | `BuzzASR/armenian` (Whisper large-v3 full fine-tune) | best published numbers among open models (card: FLEURS WER 11.9 %, Common Voice WER 2.4 %) | ~3 GB in fp16 on an 8 GB Mac; large-v3 decoder is slower |
| `turbo-hy` | `Chillarmo/whisper-large-v3-turbo-armenian` | turbo has 4 decoder layers → much faster, ~1.6 GB | no trustworthy published WER, must measure |
| `mms` | `facebook/mms-1b-all` + Armenian adapter | CTC: fast, cannot loop or hallucinate long text | usually less accurate, no punctuation |
| `turbo-zeroshot` | `openai/whisper-large-v3-turbo` | reference point | expected to be clearly worse |

Rejected without benchmarking: small/tiny Armenian Whisper fine-tunes (published WER 40–78 %); cloud ASR
(not allowed); mlx-whisper (faster on Apple Silicon, but BuzzASR replaced Whisper's tokenizer, which
mlx-whisper does not support. Worth revisiting for `turbo-hy`).

Measured on 40 Google FLEURS Armenian test clips (`eval/get_fleurs.py`), M1 8 GB, fp16 on the GPU (MPS):

| model | CER | WER | ASR time mean / p90 | GPU memory |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `buzz` | **4.6 %** | **11.6 %** | 2.76 / 2.98 s | 3.5 GB |
| `mms` | 5.2 % | 21.9 % | **0.61 / 0.74 s** | 2.3 GB |
| `turbo-hy` | 12.6 % | 45.6 % | 2.44 / 2.73 s | 2.2 GB |
| `turbo-zeroshot` | 21.5 % | 68.4 % | 2.37 / 2.68 s | — |

**Default: `buzz`**, the most accurate. Trade-off, stated honestly: it is not fast. Its ASR p90 (2.98 s) is above
the ~2.5 s we first aimed for, so total latency sits close to the 5 s limit, and it uses the most memory.
`mms` is the fallback (`--asr mms`): almost the same character error rate at a quarter of the time, but twice
the word errors and no punctuation. FLEURS is read speech; buzz may have trained on FLEURS train, so its lead on
spontaneous speech is checked in live tests (DECISIONS.md). Results: `eval/results/asr_results.csv`.

### Translation: NLLB-200 distilled 600M, CTranslate2 int8, on CPU
```

| Time per phrase | **0.25 s** | 2.76 s | 2.44 s |

- **Speed:** it's about 11× faster than buzz, so most of the 5 s budget is now free. It runs on the CPU, which was measured as fast as the GPU for this model.
- **Licence:** CC-BY-4.0, so commercial use is allowed.
- **What I changed:** `livetr/asr.py` only holds this model. NeMo is installed and added to `requirements.txt`. The README model section now shows the comparison of all five models, and the removed models' results stay in `eval/results/` and git history as evidence.
- **One caveat:** FLEURS was part of its training data, as it was for buzz. The live video test will show how it handles real conversation.

**Replaying 6 FLEURS clips through the whole program:** ASR took 0.2–0.3 s per phrase. When the Mac was quiet, lines printed **1.1–1.8 s** after each phrase ended; with buzz they took about 4.2 s.

**But some phrases took up to 11 s, and that comes from your Mac, not the program.** Another of your projects (`Programs_apps/1/lesson-videos`) is generating speech with `say` in the background. On top of that, the Mac is on battery and using 4 GB of swap. Even a simple test calculation ran up to 5.8× slower than its best time. Before the second YouTube test, and before the interview:
1. Let the lesson-video job finish, or stop it.
2. Plug in the charger.
3. Close what you don't need, especially Edge. If possible, run the program from the normal Terminal app with VS Code closed.

### Assistant · 2026-10-23 21:49:41

The speaker check is done and committed (`5314902`). It confirms that the current threshold of 0.45 is right, so no code change was needed.

## Speaker threshold

I compared voice prints for all 832 pairs of FLEURS recordings that read the same sentence:

| | Full clips | 3 s cuts (like short live phrases) |
|---|---|---|
| Man vs woman, so certainly two people | at most **0.30** | at most **0.27** |
| Same gender, different people | about 0.1–0.4 | about 0.1–0.4 |
| Same person recorded twice | about **0.6–1.0** | about **0.5–0.9** |
| Gap between "different" and "same" | 0.4–0.5 | 0.4–0.5 |

0.45 sits in that gap for both clip lengths, and no man/woman pair ever reaches it. The weak spot is two similar voices of the same gender, which can come close (0.4–0.5). The video test with more speaker switches will show whether that happens in practice.

| Part | Time |
|---|---|
| First session (claude.ai, 21 Oct): plan and the first version of the code | about 4–6 h. Not in the local logs; estimated from the brief arriving at 15:31 to the first commit at 21:17 |
| This session and two shorter sessions on 22 Oct (local logs) | about 2–3 h of active chat |
| Your own work outside the chat (BlackHole, Audio MIDI, videos, reading) | not measurable, perhaps 1–2 h |
| **Total so far** | **roughly 7–11 h of the 20 h** |

Treat this as an estimate: only the chat timestamps can be measured exactly.

|---|---|
